Hence for \(n\) large, \(\varphi_n\) is an \((F,\eps_0)\)-model of \(G\)---note that \eqref{eq:modelmult} and \eqref{eq:modelsep} refer only to the multiplication table of \(F\) inside \(G\), which is faithfully copied by the generators of \(H\).  This contradicts the choice of \((F,\eps_0)\).  Therefore \(H\) is not sofic.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Kazhdan covers]\label{thm:kcover}%
\leanverified{\leanmod{Kazhdan/ShalomFinitePresentation}{exists_presented_kazhdan_cover,exists_finitelyPresented_kazhdan_cover}\leanmod{Covers/KazhdanCover}{exists_kazhdan_finitelyPresented_cover_of_not_isSofic}\leannote{Shalom's theorem is proved internally, not cited.}}%
Let \(G\) be a nonsofic group with property~\((T)\).  Then there is an infinite finitely presented property-\((T)\) group \(H\) that is not sofic, together with a surjection \(H\onto G\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Property-\((T)\) groups are finitely generated, and \(G\) is infinite as before.  By Shalom's theorem \cite{Shalom00}, \(G\) is a quotient of a finitely presented property-\((T)\) group: fix a surjection \(\theta:P\onto G\) with \(P\) finitely presented and Kazhdan.  Choose \((F,\eps_0)\) as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:table}, and for each \(g\in F\cup F\!\cdot\!F\) choose a lift \(\tilde g\in P\) with \(\theta(\tilde g)=g\), taking \(\tilde 1=1\).  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kquotient}
 H=P\,\big/\,\bigl\langle\!\bigl\langle\,
 \tilde g\,\tilde h\,\widetilde{gh}^{\,-1}
 \ :\ g,h\in F
 \,\bigr\rangle\!\bigr\rangle .
\end{equation}
Then \(H\) is finitely presented (finitely many new relators over the finitely presented \(P\)) and has property~\((T)\) (a quotient of \(P\)).  Each relator lies in \(\ker\theta\), since \(\theta(\tilde g\tilde h\widetilde{gh}^{\,-1})=gh(gh)^{-1}=1\); hence \(\theta\) descends to a surjection \(H\onto G\), and \(H\) is infinite.  Writing \(y_g\in H\) for the image of \(\tilde g\), we have \(y_1=1\), \(y_gy_h=y_{gh}\) for \(g,h\in F\), and \(y_g\ne y_h\) for distinct \(g,h\in F\) (their images in \(G\) differ).  If \(H\) were sofic, then \(\varphi_n(g)=\sigma_n(y_g)\) would be an \((F,\eps_0)\)-model of \(G\) for large \(n\), exactly as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:table}, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:C}]
The first two assertions are Theorems~\ref{thm:table} and~\ref{thm:kcover}.  For the final assertion, apply Theorem~\ref{thm:kcover} to the infinite finitely generated property-\((T)\) nonsofic group \(L^\times\) of Theorem~\ref{thm:B}, over any fixed finite field; the resulting \(H\) surjects onto \(L^\times\), and composing with the inverses of the isomorphisms \(\Theta_{\mathcal C}\) of Theorem~\ref{thm:B}(ii) exhibits a quotient of \(H\) isomorphic to each group listed there.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Effectivity]\label{rem:effective}
The covers are effective in the logical sense: nonsoficity of \(G\) is equivalent, by Lemma~\ref{lem:models}, to the existence of a finite witness \((F,\eps_0)\), and any witness yields \(H\) by \eqref{eq:HF} or \eqref{eq:kquotient}.  The proof establishes the existence of a witness without computing one---no algorithm or complexity bound is claimed---and no claim is made that \(L^\times\) itself is finitely presented.
\end{remark}

\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:remarks}

\begin{remark}[The trust surface of Theorem~\ref{thm:A}]\label{rem:scope-final}
Theorem~\ref{thm:A} is independent of \(\GL_r=\EL_r\) and of \(K_1(L)=0\): the memberships of \(u\) and \(z\) are explicit factorizations, the remaining memberships follow from the Whitehead identity and the explicit commutator certificates of the witness pair, and the only analytic inequality proved in the paper is the co-area estimate of Lemma~\ref{lem:coarea}---the approximation-theoretic depth resides in the cited theorems of Kun and Kun--Thom.  The argument concerns permutation models only; what it leaves open for unitary ones is the subject of Remark~\ref{rem:hyperlinear}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The witness and hyperlinearity]\label{rem:hyperlinear}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Leavitt/ElementaryNoFiniteQuotients}{elGen_mul,elGen_commutator,exists_ne_zero_mem_elementaryKernel,elementaryGroup_finite_quotient_trivial,exists_third_index}\leanmod{Endpoint/StructuralProfile}{rankFour_exists_third_index,universalLeavittEL4_finite_quotient_trivial,universalLeavittEL4_no_finite_quotient,eq_top_of_permHom_trivial,universalLeavittEL4_no_properFiniteIndex}\leanmod{Sofic/HyperlinearMetric}{permMatrix_row_sq,permMatrix_dist_sq_eq,permMatrix_normalized_dist_sq_eq}\leanmod{Sofic/Hyperlinear}{hsDistSq,permMatrix_hsDistSq,hammingDistance_inv,HyperlinearModel,IsHyperlinear,permMatrix_mem_unitaryGroup,isHyperlinear_of_isSofic,isHyperlinear_of_finite,not_isSofic_of_not_isHyperlinear}}{Formalized: the triviality of every finite quotient, for the general ring hypotheses and for the witness, and the absence of a proper subgroup of finite index.  Not formalized: the profinite completion and the Malcev step, both classical.}%
Soficity implies hyperlinearity \cite{ElekSzabo05}, and whether the converse holds is Question~3.4 of \cite{Pestov08}: \emph{is every hyperlinear group sofic?}  So long as no nonsofic group was known, the question had nothing to be tested against.  Theorem~\ref{thm:A} supplies one.  If the witness is hyperlinear the two classes differ; if it is not, no approximation property of this kind survives it.  Hyperlinearity should not be confused with the Connes embedding problem for tracial von Neumann algebras, which was resolved in the negative by the \(\mathrm{MIP}^*=\mathrm{RE}\) theorem \cite{JNVWY20}.

The witness is not a neutral test object.  It contains the corner copy of \(V\) of Remark~\ref{rem:fullV}, hence the standard copy of \(F\), and hyperlinearity passes to subgroups; so a proof that the witness is hyperlinear would in particular prove \(F\) hyperlinear, and even the soficity of \(F\) is a long-standing open question.

One direction is elementary and is recorded here because it isolates what the open direction is not about.  Soficity implies hyperlinearity because the two metrics are the same metric: for permutations \(\sigma,\tau\) of a finite set \(Y\), the permutation matrices satisfy \(\norm{P_\sigma-P_\tau}_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=2\abs{\set{y\suchthat\sigma y\ne\tau y}}\), so after normalizing by \(\abs Y\) the squared Hilbert--Schmidt distance is exactly \(2\distH(\sigma,\tau)\).  Every estimate of Definition~\ref{def:sofic} transports through that identity with the error doubled and square-rooted, and \(\sigma\mapsto P_\sigma\) is a genuine homomorphism, so the passage itself creates no defect.  The content of Question~3.4 is entirely in the converse.

The heuristic offered in \cite{Pestov08} for a positive answer runs through Malcev's theorem---a finitely generated subgroup of \(U(n)\) is residually finite, hence sofic---and so covers the groups that are linear.  The witness is as far from linear as a group can be.  \emph{Every homomorphism from \(\EL_r(L)\) to a finite group is trivial}, whenever \(L\) is infinite, every nonzero element of \(L\) divides \(1\) on both sides, and \(r\geq3\).  The proof is three commutators and a division.  For \(i\ne j\) the map \(a\mapsto x_{ij}(a)\) carries addition to multiplication, so a finite target must kill some \(a\ne0\); the Steinberg identity of Lemma~\ref{lem:whitehead} moves that \(a\) into a second root subgroup as \(ab\) and into a third as \(cab\); and strong division writes an arbitrary \(d\) as \((du)\,a\,v\), so the third root subgroup dies entirely.  The generators die with it.

Consequently the witness has no proper subgroup of finite index, no nontrivial action on a finite set, and trivial profinite completion; and by Malcev's theorem again, no nontrivial finite-dimensional linear representation over any field, since the image would be a finitely generated linear group inheriting the absence of finite quotients.  Nonsoficity here is therefore not the shadow of some coarser failure of residual structure---there is no residual structure at all.  Whatever settles Question~3.4 for this group, a linear model will not be what does it.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The separation constant is a convention on one side only]\label{rem:phasecollapse}%
\leanverified{\leanmod{Sofic/HyperlinearAmplification}{tensorPow,tensorPow_mul,tensorPow_smul,trace_tensorPow}\leanmod{Sofic/HyperlinearUltraproduct}{hsLengthSq_conjTranspose_mul,UniversalHyperlinear,hsLengthSq_ge_of_separated,exists_hyperlinearApproximation_of_isHyperlinear,exists_hyperlinearEmbedding_of_approximation}\leanmod{Sofic/HyperlinearScalar}{hsNormSq,ofReal_sum_normSq,ofReal_hsNormSq,hsNormSq_sub_smul_one}}%
Remark~\ref{rem:amplification} made the sofic separation constant a convention by taking tensor powers.  The unitary computation is the same computation and is if anything cleaner.  Write \(\tau\) for the trace normalized by \(\abs Y\).  For unitaries \(A,B\) the squared normalized Hilbert--Schmidt distance is a single trace,
\[
 \norm{A-B}^2_{\mathrm{HS}}=2-2\Re\tau(AB^*),
\]
because each of \(\tau(AA^*)\) and \(\tau(BB^*)\) is \(1\); and the \(k\)-fold tensor power multiplies traces, \(\tau\bigl(A^{\otimes k}(B^{\otimes k})^*\bigr)=\tau(AB^*)^k\), so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hsamplify}
 \norm{A^{\otimes k}-B^{\otimes k}}^2_{\mathrm{HS}}=2-2\Re\bigl(\tau(AB^*)^k\bigr).
\end{equation}
And yet the amplification fails.  A scalar enters the tensor power as its \(k\)-th power, so the \(k\)-th power identifies any two unitaries differing by a \(k\)-th root of unity.  Take \(A=1\) and \(B=i\cdot1\) on any nonempty model: both are unitary, \(\tau(AB^*)=-i\), and \eqref{eq:hsamplify} gives the maximal separation \(2\) at \(k=1\) and the value \(0\) at \(k=4\), where the two tensor powers are literally equal.  Permutation models cannot exhibit this, because \(P_\sigma P_\tau^{-1}\) is again a permutation matrix and its normalized trace is the proportion of fixed points---a real number in \([0,1]\).  In Hamming a small trace means few agreements; over the unitaries a trace can be small because the agreements \emph{cancel}, and tensoring recombines the phases.

The repair is to tensor with the conjugate rather than with a further copy.  The map \(A\mapsto A\otimes\bar A\) is again multiplicative and unitary, and \(\tau(A\otimes\bar A)=\abs{\tau(A)}^2\) is nonnegative, so
\[
 \norm{(A\otimes\bar A)^{\otimes k}-(B\otimes\bar B)^{\otimes k}}^2_{\mathrm{HS}}
 =2-2\abs{\tau(AB^*)}^{2k},
\]
which runs to the maximum \(2\) as soon as \(\abs{\tau(AB^*)}<1\).  That hypothesis is not removable, and the reason is an identity rather than an estimate: for a unitary \(U\) and any scalar \(c\),
\[
 \norm{U-c\cdot1}^2_{\mathrm{HS}}=1-2\Re\bigl(\bar c\,\tau(U)\bigr)+\abs c^2 ,
 \qquad\text{so}\qquad
 \norm{U-\tau(U)\cdot1}^2_{\mathrm{HS}}=1-\abs{\tau(U)}^2 .
\]
The trace defect \emph{is} the squared distance to the scalars.  Hence \(\abs{\tau(U)}\leq1\) always, with equality exactly when \(U=\tau(U)\cdot1\)---the collapse again, now with a modulus of continuity: a unitary with \(\abs{\tau}\geq1-\delta\) sits within \(\sqrt\delta\) of a scalar, so a phase can be propagated through products and constrained by separation at every power.  The transport that propagation needs is elementary and is recorded alongside: the norm is unchanged by unitary multiplication on either side and by conjugate transposition, it scales by \(\abs c^2\), it obeys \(\norm{A+B}^2\leq2\norm A^2+2\norm B^2\), and---Cauchy--Schwarz against the identity---\(\abs{\tau(C)}^2\leq\norm C^2\), so a bound on a matrix difference is a bound on the difference of traces.  In model terms the bad case is explicit---\(A B^*\) is a scalar precisely when the model sends some element of \(F\!\cdot\!F^{-1}\) to a scalar.

So the separation constant of Definition~\ref{def:sofic} is a convention outright, while its unitary counterpart is a convention relative to a scalar-freeness hypothesis on the model.  Nothing here decides whether the unitary conventions agree; what it does is remove the standard proof from the table and name what a replacement must supply.  The definition used in Remark~\ref{rem:hyperlinear} is the strong one, which is the one soficity implies, so the implication recorded there and the reading \(\neg\text{hyperlinear}\Rightarrow\neg\text{sofic}\) are unaffected.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[Why the unitary separation constant is harder than the permutation one]\label{rem:nonscalar}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Sofic/HyperlinearNonScalar}{permMatrixC_conjTranspose,normTrace_permMatrix,NonScalarModel,IsHyperlinearNonScalar}\leanmod{Sofic/PhaseOrder}{re_pow_max_ge,re_pow_max_sharp,re_nonpos_of_pow_three_eq_one,re_nonpos_of_pow_four_eq_one}\leanmod{Sofic/PhasePropagation}{normTrace_smul_one,normTrace_sub,hsNormSq_pow_succ_sub_le,hsNormSq_pow_four_sub_le}}{Formalized: both inclusions, and the arithmetic on the unit circle.  Not formalized: Radulescu's theorem, quoted from the literature, which is what makes the two classes coincide.}%
The hypothesis isolated in Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} is a property a model either has or lacks, so it names a class.  Call a unitary model \emph{non-scalar} with constant \(\delta\) when \(\abs{\tau(u_gu_h^*)}^2\leq1-\delta\) for distinct \(g,h\) in the test set, and call a group non-scalar hyperlinear when every finite test set admits such models of every multiplicative accuracy, with \(\delta\) depending on the test set alone.  This asks more than Definition~\ref{def:sofic}'s unitary counterpart, which bounds only \(\Re\tau\) and so tolerates \(u_gu_h^*\approx\pm i\).  Both inclusions hold:
\[
 \text{sofic}\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{non-scalar hyperlinear}\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{hyperlinear}.
\]
The first holds because a permutation matrix cannot hide a phase: \(\tau(P_\pi)\) is the proportion of fixed points of \(\pi\), so transporting a sofic model gives \(\tau(u_gu_h^*)=1-\distH(\varphi h,\varphi g)\), a real number in \([0,1]\) that separation forces below \(\eps\).  The second is the conjugate-double amplification of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse}: \(k\) tensored copies of \(A\otimes\bar A\) carry the bound \(1-\delta\) to separation \(2-2(1-\delta)^k\), and inflate the multiplicative error only by \(2k\), which asking for accuracy \(\eps/2(k+1)\) at the outset absorbs.

Which scalars a strong model can produce is settled by arithmetic on the unit circle.  Suppose \(u_gu_h^*\) is close to a scalar \(\zeta\), \(\abs\zeta=1\).  Writing \(w=gh^{-1}\), approximate multiplicativity carries the closeness to the powers, \(u_{w^l}\approx\zeta^l\), and separation confines every \(\zeta^l\) with \(w^l\neq1\) to the left half plane.  No point of the unit circle survives that for four powers:
\[
 \max\bigl(\Re\zeta,\ \Re\zeta^2,\ \Re\zeta^3,\ \Re\zeta^4\bigr)\ \geq\ \tfrac3{10}.
\]
Writing \(x=\Re\zeta\), the four real parts are the Chebyshev polynomials \(x\), \(2x^2-1\), \(4x^3-3x\), \(8x^4-8x^2+1\), and the minimum of their maximum is \((\sqrt5-1)/4\approx0.30902\), attained where the second and third cross.  That crossing is exact: with \(\varphi\) the golden ratio and \(x=-\varphi/2\), the identity \(4x^3-2x^2-3x+1=0\) follows from \(\varphi^2=\varphi+1\), and both polynomials take the value \((\sqrt5-1)/4\) there while the first and fourth take \(-(1+\sqrt5)/4\).  So the constant \(3/10\) is within three percent of optimal and cannot be replaced by \(1/3\).  Four powers are needed and no fewer, and the exceptions are exactly the roots of unity of order at most four: if \(\zeta^3=1\) or \(\zeta^4=1\) with \(\zeta\neq1\) then \(\Re\zeta\leq0\), so \(-1\), \(\pm i\) and the primitive cube roots keep all their nontrivial powers in the closed left half plane.  The propagation itself is uniform, with an absolute constant.  Write \(\tau=\tau(U)\).  The identity \(U^{l+1}-\tau^{l+1}=U^l(U-\tau)+\tau(U^l-\tau^l)\), together with the fact that left multiplication by the unitary \(U^l\) and scaling by \(\tau\) (of modulus at most one) are norm-nonincreasing, gives \(a_{l+1}\leq2a_1+2a_l\) for \(a_l=\norm{U^l-\tau^l\cdot1}^2\), hence
\[
 \norm{U^4-\tau^4\cdot1}^2\ \leq\ 22\,\bigl(1-\abs{\tau}^2\bigr).
\]
Four steps is a fixed number, chosen before any accuracy is, so the factor \(2\) per step is harmless and no square roots enter.  Feeding this into the circle bound---which survives a modulus merely close to one---yields: \emph{if \(\abs{\tau(U)}^2\geq1-10^{-6}\) then \(\Re\tau(U^l)\geq1/4\) for some \(1\leq l\leq4\).}  In a model separated as in Definition~\ref{def:sofic}'s unitary counterpart every \(u_{w^l}\) with \(w^l\neq1\) has \(\Re\tau\leq\eps/2\), so once \(\eps<1/2\) no element with \(w,w^2,w^3,w^4\) all nontrivial can be sent within \(10^{-6}\) of a scalar.  The last comparison, between \(u_{w^l}\) and \(u_w^{\,l}\), is the same recursion with the model's own defect in place of the scalar one: \(u_{w^{l+1}}-u_w^{\,l+1}=(u_{w^lw}-u_{w^l}u_w)+(u_{w^l}-u_w^{\,l})u_w\), so the deficit is at most \(14\eps\) by the fourth step, and \(u_1\) itself is within \(\eps\) of the identity because \(u_1-u_1u_1=u_1(1-u_1)\).  Assembling: \emph{if \(\eps\leq10^{-4}\) and \(w,w^2,w^3,w^4\) all lie in the test set and are all nontrivial, then \(\abs{\tau(u_w)}^2<1-10^{-6}\).}  A separated unitary model cannot put a phase on an element of order five or more, and by the root-of-unity computation above the scalars that remain available are exactly those of order at most four.  The obstruction of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} is therefore confined to small torsion---which is precisely where the lamp groups of Theorem~\ref{thm:A}'s wreath-product relatives live.

The exception at order two is not available twice.  Let \(g,h\) be nontrivial involutions with \(g,h,gh\) in the test set and \(gh\neq1\).  If both were sent within \(10^{-6}\) of a scalar then each scalar would be a near-square-root of one with nonpositive real part, hence near \(-1\); their product would be near \(+1\); and \(\tau(u_{gh})\) is near that product, contradicting the separation of \(gh\) from the identity.  So \emph{a separated model cannot put a phase on two involutions at once}.  In an elementary abelian \(2\)-group---where every nontrivial element is an involution and the product of two distinct ones is again nontrivial---at most one element can carry a phase.  The escape hatch that order two leaves open is therefore closed as soon as the group in question is a lamp group, which is the only case in which it was ever going to matter.

The two classes in fact coincide, but not for an elementary reason.  By Radulescu's theorem---Theorem~8.5 of \cite{Pestov08}, from \cite{Radulescu08}---a countable group is hyperlinear exactly when its group von Neumann algebra embeds into \(R^\omega\), and restricting a trace-preserving embedding to the group unitaries gives \(\tau(u_g)=\delta_{g,e}\) on the nose, hence non-scalar models with \(\delta\) as close to \(1\) as one likes.  What the two inclusions above record is the proof-theoretic asymmetry this hides.  On the permutation side the corresponding normalization is Theorem~3.5 of \cite{Pestov08}, whose separation constant \(1/4\) is made arbitrary by the amplification of Remark~\ref{rem:amplification}.  Remark~3.7 of \cite{Pestov08} asserts the same refinement for the unitary characterization of its Theorem~3.6, without proof---and Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} shows that the amplification cannot supply it, since the tensor power identifies unitaries differing by a root of unity.  The unitary refinement rests on the von Neumann algebra theorem; the elementary route available on the permutation side provably does not exist on the unitary side.  The asymmetry is visible at the level of the ultraproducts themselves.  The unitary metric ultraproduct \(\prod_{\mathcal U}U(Y_i)\) is built exactly as its permutation counterpart is---null sequences are those whose squared Hilbert--Schmidt length vanishes along \(\mathcal U\), and the three closure properties follow from \(\norm{A+B}^2\leq2\norm A^2+2\norm B^2\) together with unitary invariance on both sides, a factor \(2\) per step being harmless for a limit.  Conjugation invariance makes the null sequences normal, so the quotient is a group---the universal hyperlinear group over the family---and the metric it carries is the one the models are measured in, since \(\norm{w^{*}v-1}^2=\norm{v-w}^2\) for unitary \(w\), the exact counterpart of the identity \(\abs{q^{-1}p}=\distH(p,q)\) that plays the same role for permutations.  One direction of the bridge transfers intact, and it applies to hyperlinear groups rather than to a hypothesis: for a countable group, exhausting by finite test sets while shrinking the accuracy turns the local definition into a sequence of models, and such a sequence defines an injective homomorphism into the quotient: the multiplicative defect of a product is exactly the length of the corresponding sequence, by the identity above, and injectivity is the estimate that in a separated model every nontrivial test element has length at least \(2-\eps-1/50\).  Neither step uses amplification, which is why both survive.  What does not transfer is the converse: the permutation version turns an embedding back into a model of full separation by amplification, and that is the step Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} closes here.  What is elementary is exactly the two inclusions: soficity gives the trace normalization directly, because a permutation matrix cannot hide a phase, and the normalization amplifies back to full separation.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[A candidate on the other side]\label{rem:thomK}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Sofic/DivisibleInvisible}{invPowSubgroup,pruferSubgroup_divisible,prufer_map_eq_zero,pruferSubgroup_nontrivial}\leanmod{Sofic/MonomialModel}{monomialMatrix,hammingDistance_le_hsDistSq_monomial}\leanmod{Sofic/HeisenbergCentre}{invPowSubring,heis_centre_map_eq_one,Heis.commutator_mem_center,Heis.commutator_generators}\leanmod{Sofic/DivisibleInvisible}{map_eq_one_of_mem_divisible_subgroup}}{Formalized: the Heisenberg centre, the Pr\"ufer centre and its invisibility to finite groups, and the two monomial inequalities.  Not formalized: Thom's construction and its properties, quoted from the literature.}%
Theorem~\ref{thm:A} supplies a nonsofic group to test Question~3.4 against.  A candidate on the other side has been available since 2008 and deserves to be named here, because the distinction it turns on is the one of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse}.  Let \(K_0(\Z[1/p])\) be de~Cornulier's group of block-unipotent matrices in \(\operatorname{SL}_8(\Z[1/p])\), and \(K=K_0(\Z[1/p])/\Z\) its quotient by the copy of \(\Z\) inside its centre \(\Z[1/p]\).  Then \(K\) is finitely presented, Kazhdan and non-Hopfian, hence not residually finite \cite{Cornulier07}; and \(K\) is \emph{hyperlinear} \cite[Prop.~3.2]{Thom10}, because \(K_0(\Z[1/p])\) is residually finite and hyperlinearity passes to quotients by central subgroups.  The structural core of that description---a group whose centre is a copy of the coefficient ring, so that quotienting by a copy of \(\Z\) inside it leaves a divisible centre---does not have to be taken on trust.  The Heisenberg group \(\Heis(R)=R^3\) with \((a,b,c)(a',b',c')=(a+a',b+b',c+c'+ab')\) has centre exactly its third coordinate, so \(\Heis(R)\) has centre \((R,+)\) on the nose.  That is not de Cornulier's \(K_0(\Z[1/p])\), which must also be finitely presented and Kazhdan---\(\Heis(\Z[1/p])\) is neither---but it is the shape of the construction, verified rather than quoted.  Two further steps make that shape usable: the centre is not merely \emph{described} by the third coordinate but \emph{is} the additive group of the ring---the map \(c\mapsto(0,0,c)\) is injective, carries addition to multiplication, and has the centre as its exact range---and central elements stay central in a quotient by a central subgroup, so the centre of \(\Heis(R)/Z\) contains \((R,+)/Z\).  With \(R=\Z[1/p]\) and \(Z\) the copy of \(\Z\), that is the Pr\"ufer group below, and the chain closes: \(\Z[1/p]\) is a subring of \(\Q\), so \(\Heis(\Z[1/p])\) makes sense; its centre is \(\Z[1/p]\), which is \emph{not} divisible; the quotient by \(\Z\) makes it divisible, because dividing by \(p\) stays inside and dividing by anything prime to \(p\) works modulo \(\Z\); and Lagrange then kills it in every finite quotient.  So every homomorphism from \(\Heis(\Z[1/p])/\Z\) to a finite group is trivial on the image of the centre---the mechanism of this remark in a group built from scratch, with no representation theory and nothing quoted.  That group is of course not Thom's, but the reason is worth getting right.  It is \emph{not} residually finite---the displayed statement is exactly a failure of residual finiteness, since the central image is nontrivial and every finite quotient kills it, which is the same failure that makes \(K\) interesting.  The reason it is no candidate is that \(\Heis(R)\) is \(2\)-step nilpotent, every commutator being central, so the group is nilpotent, hence amenable, hence sofic---for reasons having nothing to do with its centre.  That is also what distinguishes \(K_0(\Z[1/p])\): being Kazhdan it is as far from amenable as a group can be, which is precisely why \emph{its} central quotient can be a candidate when this one cannot.  The obstruction to using a Heisenberg group here is nilpotence, not residual finiteness.

Isolating that reverses the usual emphasis, and it is worth saying which half of a candidate's profile is cheap.  A nontrivial \emph{divisible} subgroup obstructs residual finiteness outright---every homomorphism to a finite group kills it, so no finite quotient separates any of its elements from the identity, and nothing about centres or about the ambient group is used.  So failing residual finiteness, which a candidate must do since residually finite groups are sofic, costs only a planted divisible subgroup, and a divisible centre is the cheapest way to plant one.  What such a group must \emph{also} be is non-amenable, since amenable groups are sofic; and that is the expensive ingredient, the one Kazhdan's property supplies and a nilpotent group cannot.

Whether Kazhdan's property is \emph{needed} is a separate question, and this remark does not settle it.  De Cornulier's group was built to be finitely presented and non-Hopfian, for which property~(T) is doing real work; the profile isolated here asks only for a hyperlinear group that is non-amenable and carries a nontrivial divisible central subgroup with the extension non-split.  Nothing above rules out meeting that profile by other means---a central extension of some non-amenable residually finite group by \(\Z[1/p]\), quotiented by \(\Z\), would have the same centre for the same reason---and nothing above supplies one either.  The gap between the profile and a group meeting it is not addressed here, and is the natural place for the next attempt.

One clause of the profile the Heisenberg construction does settle, and by computation rather than argument: the extension is non-split, because the centre is \emph{generated by commutators}.  Explicitly \([(1,0,0),(0,1,0)]=(0,0,1)\), so the central element \(1\) is a commutator; the centre therefore meets the commutator subgroup nontrivially whenever \(1\neq0\) in \(R\), and no direct-product decomposition separates it off.  That matters because a split extension would be useless---the divisible centre would come away and buy nothing.  What the construction cannot supply is the non-amenability, every commutator being central; a group meeting the whole profile has to obtain its centre this way while failing to be nilpotent.

Its centre contains the Pr\"ufer group \(\Z[1/p]/\Z\), which is divisible, and a divisible group has no nontrivial homomorphism to a finite group: write \(d=e^{\abs B}\) and apply Lagrange.  Note that \(\Z[1/p]\) itself is \emph{not} divisible---only \(p\) has been inverted---so the divisibility is a property of the quotient and is proved by two moves.  Dividing by \(p\) stays inside \(\Z[1/p]\) exactly, since \(c/p^N=p\cdot(c/p^{N+1})\); dividing by an \(n\) prime to \(p\) is possible only modulo \(\Z\), by B\'ezout: choose \(u,v\) with \(un+vp^N=1\), and then \(c/p^N-n\cdot(uc/p^N)=vc\in\Z\).  Writing \(n=p^k m\) with \(\gcd(m,p)=1\) and iterating the first move \(k\) times reduces the general case to these two.  The same argument applies to \(\Q/\Z\), of which the Pr\"ufer group is the \(p\)-primary part.  So every finite quotient of \(K\) kills that centre, and by Malcev no finite-dimensional representation sees it either: the approximations are forced to be projective.  Its soficity is open---in Thom's words, ``we were not able to show that the group \(K\) is sofic''.  A proof that \(K\) is not sofic would answer Question~3.4 in the negative.

What blocks the passage is exactly the phase.  Thom's microstates for \(K\) are not unitary in a shapeless way: they lie in the monomial group \(T_n\rtimes S_n\subseteq U(n)\), permutation matrices carrying diagonal phases, assembled from the twisted group algebras \(\mathbb{C}_{\beta\circ\alpha}K_1(\Z/q\Z)\) attached to the central extension.  Their traces are near zero for nontrivial elements because the phases \emph{cancel}.  Soficity would require the same smallness with the torus removed---and there, by the identity recorded in Remark~\ref{rem:nonscalar}, the normalized trace of a permutation matrix is its proportion of fixed points, a real number in \([0,1]\) that can only be small by genuine scarcity of agreements.  So the gap between what is proved about \(K\) and what Question~3.4 asks of it is precisely the gap between a trace that vanishes by cancellation and one that vanishes by disagreement, and Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} shows that tensor amplification cannot close it.

Two inequalities measure that gap exactly.  Write \(A=A(d,\sigma)\) for the monomial matrix with permutation \(\sigma\) and unimodular phases \(d\).  First, the permutation part inherits the whole multiplicative defect: two monomial matrices whose permutations disagree at \(i\) differ in two entries of row \(i\), each of modulus one, so
\[
 2\,\distH(\sigma,\tau)\ \leq\ \norm{A(d,\sigma)-A(e,\tau)}^2_{\mathrm{HS}} .
\]
The underlying permutations of a monomial model are therefore an approximately multiplicative map into \(\Sym(Y)\), with no loss at all.  Second, the trace is bounded by the fixed-point fraction of that permutation:
\[
 \abs{\tau(A(d,\sigma))}^2\ \leq\ \bigl(1-\distH(\sigma,1)\bigr)^2 .
\]
So a small trace is \emph{permitted} by many fixed points and never \emph{caused} by them.  The purest instance is a two-point model with the identity permutation and phases \(1,-1\): the trace is \(0\) while nothing whatever is moved.  A permutation model cannot imitate it, because there the normalized trace \emph{is} the fixed-point fraction.

It would be natural to conclude that a monomial model is a sofic model exactly when its underlying permutations are already almost fixed-point free, so that cancellation is worthless.  That conclusion is \emph{false}, and the untwisting identity below says why: what the untwisted model needs is scarcity of points that are fixed \emph{and trivially phased}, so a phase that never vanishes on \(\Fix(\sigma)\) untwists to a fixed-point-free permutation however little \(\sigma\) moves.  Remark~\ref{rem:untwistsep} exhibits the two-point identity permutation with phases \(\pm i\): the trace is \(0\), the permutation part moves nothing, and the untwisted model is \emph{maximally} separated.  What decides the matter is not how much the permutation moves but the torsion of the phase.  For \(K\) the missing step is exactly the removal of that torus, and what removal requires can be said exactly: a monomial model whose data are multiplicative \emph{combinatorially}---permutation and phase agreeing outside density \(\eps\)---and separated by scarcity of points where permutation and phase both agree, \emph{is} a permutation model, on \(m\) times as many points.  Those two conditions are precisely the two quantities the untwisting identities below compute, so the criterion is not a reformulation but a statement of what a model must satisfy.  Its converse holds trivially---a permutation model is monomial data with a single phase---so
\[
 \text{sofic}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{combinatorial monomial approximability.}
\]
Requiring the phases to be multiplicative \emph{combinatorially}, agreeing as elements of \(\Z/m\) outside a small set, therefore adds nothing at all to soficity.  What the open question is about is the \emph{metric} alternative, in which nearby phases count as equal; and the two prices of a scalar below say exactly how far apart those two demands are.

Removal is always available as a \emph{construction}, and its price is exact.  Phases valued in \(\Z/m\) are absorbed by a second coordinate: \((y,j)\mapsto(\sigma y,\,j+d(y))\) is an honest permutation of \(Y\times\Z/m\), the standard embedding of \((\Z/m)^Y\rtimes\Sym(Y)\) into \(\Sym(Y\times\Z/m)\).  Under it,
\begin{align*}
 \distH\bigl(\widetilde{(d,\sigma)},\widetilde{(e,\tau)}\bigr)
 &=\frac{\abs{\set{y\suchthat\sigma y\ne\tau y\ \text{or}\ d(y)\ne e(y)}}}{\abs Y},\\
 \distH\bigl(\widetilde{(d,\sigma)},1\bigr)
 &=1-\frac{\abs{\set{y\suchthat\sigma y=y\ \text{and}\ d(y)=0}}}{\abs Y}.
\end{align*}
The second identity is what a sofic model needs, and it says the separation is scarcity of \emph{trivially phased} fixed points---not of fixed points.  Cancellation among the phases, which is what makes a monomial trace small while moving nothing, therefore buys nothing at order two and everything at order four; Remark~\ref{rem:untwistsep} makes both halves of that precise.  The first is the price: a phase difference invisible to the Hilbert--Schmidt metric---two nearby roots of unity---is fully visible in Hamming.  So untwisting trades a metric defect for a combinatorial one, and the two identities say precisely what must be traded.  A scalar factor is the extreme case: it leaves the permutation part untouched and changes every phase, so it costs the whole of \(Y\)---which is Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} again, in the monomial category.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[Exact infeasibility is a determinant, not a gap]\label{rem:scalarcocycle}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Sofic/ScalarCocycle}{pow_card_eq_one_of_scalarCommute,hsDistSq_monomial_const,hammingDistance_wreathPerm_blockConst}\leanmod{Sofic/CharacterCount}{card_annihilator,hammingDistance_characterUntwist,hammingDistance_characterUntwist_generator}}{Formalized: the determinant obstruction, the clock--shift solution, the annihilator count and the untwisting defect \(1-\gcd(n,\alpha)/n\).  Not formalized: that Thom's microstates realize the generating case, which is read off his construction and quoted from the literature.}%
It is tempting to read emptiness of an \emph{exact} finite-dimensional solution locus, at every dimension, as half of a non-closure profile, with only robustness left to establish.  The scalar commutation relation shows that reading is unsound, and shows it with the cheapest witness available.  For unitaries \(U,V\) on a model of size \(n\), the relation \(UV=c\,(VU)\) forces \(c^{\,n}=1\): take determinants, \(\det U\det V=c^{\,n}\det V\det U\), and the determinants are nonzero.  So for \(c\) not a root of unity the exact locus is empty at every dimension---one line, no rigidity.  Yet the relation is exactly solvable whenever \(c\) is a \(q\)-th root of unity, on a model of size \(q\): the shift \(U_{ij}=[j=i+1]\) and the clock \(V_{ij}=[j=i]\,\zeta^{\,i}\) on \(\Z/q\) satisfy \(UV=\zeta\,(VU)\) on the nose.  Since the roots of unity are dense in the circle, every \(c\) is a limit of exactly solvable instances.  Emptiness of every exact locus therefore carries no dimension-free gap for the relaxed loci.

Two features of the witness are worth recording.  It is a \emph{monomial} pair---a permutation carrying a diagonal of phases---so the entire phenomenon lives in the group of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK}; and what it evades is a \emph{scalar} cocycle, which is exactly the configuration Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} shows tensor amplification cannot remove and Remark~\ref{rem:thomK} prices at the whole of the model when one tries to untwist it.  The same object appears in all three places, which is some evidence that it is the right object.

The two prices of a scalar can be stated exactly, and their mismatch is the whole difficulty.  Multiplying every phase of a monomial matrix by \(\zeta\) moves it in Hilbert--Schmidt by precisely \(\abs{1-\zeta}^2\), which is arbitrarily small for \(\zeta\) near one.  Shifting every phase by a nonzero constant leaves the permutation part untouched and changes the second coordinate at every point, so the untwisted permutations of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK} disagree \emph{everywhere}: the Hamming distance is exactly \(1\).  A scalar cocycle is therefore invisible to the metric that hyperlinearity uses and maximal in the metric that soficity uses.  That is why a projective model cannot simply be untwisted, and it is the precise sense in which the two approximation categories part company.

A projective model assembled from a family of characters pays a graded version of the same price.  If the phase difference is constant on each block of a partition, the untwisted permutations disagree exactly on the blocks where that constant is nonzero, so the multiplicative defect after untwisting is the \emph{proportion of characters that see the cocycle}.  The global scalar above is the one-block case, costing everything; a cocycle invisible to almost every character costs almost nothing.  That is the quantity a construction of the kind described in Remark~\ref{rem:thomK} has to control, and it is a finite count rather than an analytic estimate---indeed it is a greatest common divisor.  The characters of \(\Z/n\) are \(\beta_l(x)=e^{2\pi ilx/n}\) for \(l\in\Z/n\), and \(\beta_l\) kills \(\alpha\) exactly when \(l\alpha=0\); the map \(l\mapsto l\alpha\) has image of order \(n/\gcd(n,\alpha)\), so by Lagrange its kernel has order \(\gcd(n,\alpha)\) and
\[
 \distH\bigl(\widetilde{(d,\sigma)},\widetilde{(d+\beta_\bullet(\alpha),\sigma)}\bigr)
 \ =\ 1-\frac{\gcd(n,\alpha)}{n}
\]
on the model indexed by all \(n\) characters.  The two ends of that formula are the two readings above.  A cocycle datum invisible to almost every character has \(\gcd(n,\alpha)\) close to \(n\) and costs almost nothing.  A datum that \emph{generates}---which is the case for Thom's microstates at level \(p^k\), where the cocycle takes values generating the \(p^k\)-torsion of the Pr\"ufer centre---has \(\gcd=1\): exactly one character out of \(p^k\) kills it, the defect is \(1-p^{-k}\), and untwisting destroys the model as \(k\) grows.  So the obvious passage from Thom's projective microstates to a sofic approximation does not merely fail to be obvious.  It fails, with a computed defect.

The extreme case is worth stating on its own, because it is the obstruction in a single line.  On any nonempty model take the monomial matrices with the same permutation part \(\sigma\) and constant phases \(1\) and \(-1\).  They sit at the \emph{maximal} Hilbert--Schmidt distance \(4\), while their permutation parts are equal, so their untwisted permutations coincide and no model derived from them separates the pair at all.  Metric separation, which is what hyperlinearity supplies, therefore gives no combinatorial separation whatever, which is what soficity demands---and by the characterization above that passage is the whole of the difference between the two classes.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[No pointwise rounding of phases]\label{rem:norounding}%
\leanverified{\leanmod{Sofic/NoRounding}{circle_map_eq_zero,additiveRounding_no_separation,roundMod_not_additive,no_pointwise_rounding}}%
The characterization of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK} says that soficity is \emph{combinatorial} monomial approximability, while hyperlinearity supplies only \emph{metric} agreement of phases.  The obvious way to close that gap is to round: replace each phase, which lives in the circle, by a nearby \(m\)-th root of unity, and hope that multiplicativity survives.  It cannot, and the obstruction is a fork whose two horns exhaust the pointwise rules.

If the rounding is \emph{multiplicative}, it is trivial.  The circle is divisible and \(\Z/m\) is finite, so every homomorphism between them is zero---the two lines of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK}, applied one level up, to the group the phases live in rather than to the group being modelled.  A multiplicative rounding therefore sends every phase to \(1\); two monomial data differing only in their phases untwist to the \emph{same} permutation, and the separation such a rounding provides is exactly \(0\), for every pair of phase systems and every permutation part.

If the rounding is \emph{nearest-point}, it is not multiplicative, at every \(m\) and however fine.  The witness is uniform in \(m\): \(3/10m\) rounds down to \(0\) while its double rounds up to \(1/m\).  Refining does not help, because the failure is scale-invariant---it is the statement that the sawtooth \(x\mapsto\operatorname{round}(x)-x\) is not additive, and rounding to \(\mu_m\) is that sawtooth read at scale \(1/m\).

A pointwise rule either respects the group law or it does not, so the two horns are exhaustive.  The passage from a metric phase system to a combinatorial one, if it exists at all, therefore cannot be pointwise: it has to use the group being modelled and the model itself.  This is the shape of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} again---not a difficulty in carrying out a strategy, but a proof that the strategy does not exist---and it is why the gap priced in Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle} is not an artifact of a bad choice of \(m\).
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[The non-pointwise correction, and where it stops]\label{rem:phasecorrection}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Sofic/PhaseCorrection}{phaseCorrection_eq,phase_correctable_of_small,window_defect_eq,abs_window_defect_le}}{Formalized: the averaging identity, the lifting, the correction bound, and the windowed identity with its boundary term.  Not formalized: that almost invariant windows characterize amenability, or that amenable groups are sofic; both are quoted.}%
Remark~\ref{rem:norounding} closes every pointwise rule, and leaves exactly one thing open: a correction chosen with the whole group in view.  Carry it out.  The defect of a phase system \(d\) is the twisted \(2\)-cocycle \(c(g,h)=g\cdot d_h+d_g-d_{gh}\), and replacing \(d\) by \(d+b\) moves \(c\) by the coboundary of \(b\).  So the question is whether a cocycle with values uniformly near \(0\) is the coboundary of something near \(0\), and it is---by averaging over the group:
\[
 b_g\ =\ \frac{1}{\abs G}\sum_{k\in G}c(g,k),
 \qquad
 c\ =\ \delta b,
 \qquad
 \norm b_\infty\leq\norm c_\infty .
\]
Summing the cocycle identity over \(k\) and reindexing \(k\mapsto hk\) is the whole proof.  Two things make it work and neither is pointwise: the average is taken over \(G\), and the passage from the circle to \(\R\) is a lifting---a cochain satisfying the cocycle identity only \emph{modulo} \(\Z\), with values within \(1/6\) of \(0\), satisfies it exactly, because the identity has four terms and \(4/6<1\).

The conclusion is a dichotomy, and it is what the remark is for.  \emph{No obstruction to soficity can come from a phase defect that is uniformly small.}  Such a defect is correctable outright, by a correction no larger than itself, and the corrected phases are exactly multiplicative.  An obstruction therefore has to be a defect that is small \emph{on average over the model} and large somewhere---which is precisely the regime the Hilbert--Schmidt metric permits and the Hamming metric does not, and precisely the gap priced in Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle}.  The scalar witness there is the extreme instance: uniformly \emph{maximal} rather than uniformly small, invisible on average and fatal in Hamming.

There is a second place the argument can fail, and it is worth separating from the first.  The average above runs over all of \(G\), so it is available only for a finite group; for an infinite group one may average only over a finite window \(F\), and then the reindexing \(k\mapsto hk\)---the single step that made the proof work---no longer maps the window to itself.  What that costs is \emph{exactly} the boundary:
\[
 c(g,h)-\delta b(g,h)
 \ =\ \frac{1}{\abs F}\Bigl(\sum_{k\in hF\setminus F}c(g,k)\ -\sum_{k\in F\setminus hF}c(g,k)\Bigr),
\]
so \(\norm{c-\delta b}_\infty\leq\norm c_\infty\cdot\abs{hF\symdiff F}/\abs F\).  The group-averaged correction therefore survives to an infinite group exactly when almost invariant windows are available---that is, exactly for amenable groups, which are sofic already.  The route recovers what is known and stalls precisely at non-amenability, and the stalling is a named quantity rather than a vague difficulty.

Read together with Remark~\ref{rem:norounding} this locates the difficulty exactly, and along two independent axes.  Pointwise rules cannot work at all.  The group-averaged rule works whenever the defect is uniform \emph{and} the window is almost invariant.  So whatever separates hyperlinear from sofic, if anything does, lives in the failure of one of those two---in defects concentrated on a small part of the model, or in the non-amenability of the group.  That is a sharper statement of the open problem than ``permutations versus unitaries'', and it is where a resolution would have to begin.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[The scalar class lives in bounded cohomology]\label{rem:scalarclass}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Sofic/ScalarClass}{isScalarCocycle_scalarPart,abs_scalarPart_le,scalarPart_phaseCob,scalarClass_obstructs}}{Formalized: that the model average untwists the cocycle identity, keeps the bound, commutes with correcting, and hence obstructs.  Not formalized: Johnson's vanishing of bounded cohomology for amenable groups, which is quoted.}%
Remark~\ref{rem:phasecorrection} fences the problem but does not say what the correction has to kill.  Averaging in the other direction says it.  Average the defect over the \emph{model} rather than the group,
\[
 \hat c(g,h)\ =\ \frac{1}{\abs Y}\sum_{y\in Y}c(g,h)(y),
\]
and two things happen at once.  The action disappears---it enters the cocycle identity only as a relabelling of \(Y\), and an average over \(Y\) cannot see a relabelling---so the \emph{twisted} identity becomes the \emph{untwisted} one.  And the bound survives, \(\norm{\hat c}_\infty\leq\norm c_\infty\).  So the average of a small twisted defect is a \emph{bounded \(2\)-cocycle on \(G\) with trivial coefficients}: exactly the object bounded cohomology is about.

The identification closes because averaging commutes with correcting.  The scalar part of a coboundary is the coboundary of the scalar part, with \(\norm\beta_\infty\leq\norm b_\infty\); contraposing, \emph{if the bounded class of \(\hat c\) does not vanish then no bounded correction of \(c\) exists at all}.  The scalar obstruction therefore lives in \(H^2_b(G,\R)\).

That is not a new difficulty but the old one named.  Bounded cohomology vanishes for amenable groups and does not in general; the correction of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecorrection} succeeds exactly when the window is almost invariant; the two facts are the same fact, and the Følner boundary there is the averaging that computes the class.  It also explains why the scalar witness of Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle} is the extreme case: a constant defect \emph{is} its own scalar part, carrying no mean-zero component whatever, and it is precisely the component the Hilbert--Schmidt metric cannot see.

What this does not supply is a group whose class is non-vanishing \emph{and} forced on every model.  Soficity quantifies over finite windows and a bounded class is a statement about the whole group; any single window can be corrected by extending the correction arbitrarily, so an obstruction would have to be uniform over windows.  That is the same dimension-free gap that Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle} shows exact infeasibility does not supply, and it is not closed here.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[Rounding a character, where rounding a circle failed]\label{rem:ratchar}%
\leanverified{\leanmod{Sofic/RationalCharacter}{charEval_add,ratChar_add,ratChar_eq_div,exists_ratChar_close}}%
Remark~\ref{rem:norounding} invites a misreading, and it should be corrected here rather than left standing.  That no pointwise rule turns circle-valued phases into \(\mu_m\)-valued ones does \emph{not} mean the restriction to \(m\)-th roots of unity in the criterion above is itself an obstacle.  The failure there is a failure of \emph{freeness}, not of fineness.  A homomorphism out of the circle is pinned down everywhere by divisibility; a homomorphism out of a free abelian group is pinned down by nothing at all, since its values on a basis may be chosen arbitrarily and any choice extends.

Once the phases are exactly multiplicative---which is what a correction buys---their values on a finite window form a character of a finitely generated free abelian group, and such a character \emph{can} be rounded, multiplicatively and to finite order.  A real character of \(\Z^r\) is \(n\mapsto\sum_i n_ia_i\); replacing each \(a_i\) by \(\operatorname{round}(qa_i)/q\) gives a character with values in \(\tfrac1q\Z\), still a homomorphism on the nose, and
\[
 \Bigl|\,\textstyle\sum_i n_i\tfrac{\operatorname{round}(qa_i)}{q}-\sum_i n_ia_i\Bigr|
 \ \leq\ \frac{\sum_i\abs{n_i}}{2q}.
\]
On coefficients bounded by \(N\)---and a finite window over a finite model supplies exactly such a bound---taking \(q\) large makes the agreement as tight as one likes.

So the \(\R/\Z\) versus \(\mu_m\) distinction is not where the difficulty lives, and the criterion loses nothing by demanding finite order.  What remains hard is what Remarks~\ref{rem:phasecorrection} and~\ref{rem:scalarclass} isolate---obtaining exact multiplicativity at all---and the separation of the untwisted model, which no amount of rounding addresses.  Read with Remark~\ref{rem:norounding} the pair is sharp: rounding fails on the circle because the circle is divisible, and succeeds on a lattice because a lattice is free.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[Untwisting cannot manufacture separation]\label{rem:untwistsep}%
\leanpartial{\leanmod{Sofic/UntwistSeparation}{involution_untwist_hamming_le,card_trivially_phased_le,card_torsion_subgroup,renormalization_isHom}\leanmod{Sofic/UntwistSeparation}{phase_commutator_local,untwist_retains_commutator_offBad,card_fixed_commutator_constraint}\leanmod{Sofic/UntwistSeparation}{re_normTrace_commutator_ge,card_fixed_commutator_constraint_tight,exists_unitary_far_from_monomial,monomialMatrix_pow_card_comm,untwist_separated_of_phase_trivial,hyperlinear_forces_sofic_iff_phases_off_axis}\leanmod{Sofic/UntwistSeparation}{phase_eq_zero_of_gcd_eq_one,untwist_full_separation_witness_even,phase_weights_cancel}}{Formalized: every bound and witness stated here, including the order two, three and four cases and the coprime vanishing.  Not formalized: that Thom's construction is the compatible-modulus case, quoted from the literature.}%
The preceding remarks on correction, on the scalar class and on rational characters are all about \emph{multiplicativity}: obtaining phases that compose exactly.  None of them touches the other half of a sofic model, which is \emph{separation}---that a nontrivial element move almost every point.  This remark addresses that half, and the news is negative and quantitative.

The fixed points of the untwisted permutation of \((d,\sigma)\) are the pairs \((y,j)\) with \(\sigma y=y\) and \(d(y)=0\), so untwisting separates only to the extent that the phase avoids \(0\) on \(\Fix(\sigma)\).  But \(\Fix(\sigma)\) is partitioned by the \(m\) classes of \(d\), so some class carries at least an average share:
\[
 \exists c,\qquad
 \frac{\abs{\set{y\suchthat\sigma y=y,\ d(y)=c}}}{\abs Y}
 \ \geq\ \frac1m\cdot\frac{\abs{\Fix(\sigma)}}{\abs Y}.
\]
Renormalizing the phase by that \(c\) is a \emph{scalar} change, hence invisible to the Hilbert--Schmidt metric by the identity of Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle}, and it leaves an untwisted model of separation at most \(1-F/m\), where \(F\) is the fixed point density of \(\sigma\).  So for any single element, some scalar shift retains a \(1/m\) share of its fixed points.  That is a statement about one element's phase function, and it must not be over-read.  A shift is invisible to the Hilbert--Schmidt metric, which suggests the metric cannot rule out a bad normalization; but a renormalization of a whole \emph{model}, \(d_g\mapsto d_g+\beta(g)\), has to preserve multiplicativity, and subtracting the two multiplicativity identities gives
\[
 \beta(gh)=\beta(g)+\beta(h).
\]
The renormalization freedom is therefore \(\operatorname{Hom}(G,\Z/m)\), not one free constant per element.  Being a homomorphism into an abelian group, \(\beta\) kills commutators; so on a group in which every element is a commutator the only renormalization is the trivial one, and the phase system \emph{does} determine the untwisted separation.  The \(c\) supplied by the pigeonhole is a genuine scalar for its element, but it is not in general available as a renormalization of the model.

The same argument says something about the phases themselves, and it is sharper.  Nothing above used that \(\beta\) came from a renormalization---only that it turned products into sums.  At a point \(y\) fixed by \emph{every} element of the group the phase function \(g\mapsto d_g(y)\) is exactly that, since the action never moves \(y\) out of the way:
\[
 d_{gh}(y)=d_g(\sigma_h y)+d_h(y)=d_g(y)+d_h(y).
\]
So on a group in which every element is a commutator, the phase vanishes identically at any globally fixed point.  Such a point is then trivially phased for every element, survives untwisting as a fixed point in every fibre, and the untwisted model retains it whatever the phases are.  \emph{Phases are powerless at a globally fixed point of a perfect group.}  This is the reverse of the picture the witnesses suggest: there a well-chosen phase removed every fixed point, but that model's permutation part was the identity on a group with no perfection to exploit.

The statement just made quantifies over the whole group, and a model does not have a whole group---it has a finite window and an approximate action, hence no genuine stabilizer subgroup.  The argument survives anyway, because it is \emph{local}.  To kill the phase of a commutator \([a,b]\) at a point \(y\) one needs only
\[
 \sigma_b y=y,\qquad \sigma_{a^{-1}}y=y,\qquad \sigma_{b^{-1}}y=y,
\]
and the three lines
\[
 d_{ab}(y)=d_a(y)+d_b(y),\quad d_{a^{-1}}(y)=-d_a(y),\quad d_{b^{-1}}(y)=-d_b(y)
\]
then cancel in pairs.  Perfection of \(G\) is not used, no closure property of the window beyond naming the elements involved is used, and---slightly surprisingly---\(\sigma_a\) need not fix \(y\) at all: three of the four constituents suffice.  So in \emph{any} monomial model, at any point fixed by \(b\), \(a^{-1}\) and \(b^{-1}\), the commutator \([a,b]\) is trivially phased, and the point survives untwisting.  On a group whose elements are commutators that is a statement about exactly the elements one has to separate.

The counting the local statement needs is Bonferroni, and it simplifies: a permutation and its inverse have the same fixed points, so a point fixed by \(b,a^{-1},b^{-1}\) is just a point fixed by \(a\) and by \(b\), and there are at least \(F_a+F_b-1\) of them.  Every such point is fixed by \([a,b]\) and trivially phased there, hence survives untwisting in every fibre, so
\[
 \distH\bigl(\widetilde{(d_{[a,b]},\sigma_{[a,b]})},1\bigr)\ \leq\ 2-F_a-F_b .
\]
Read the other way: if the untwisted model separates \([a,b]\) to within \(\eps\) then \(F_a+F_b\leq1+\eps\), so \emph{at most one element of the window can fix more than half the model}.  That is the quantitative form of ``untwisting only inherits separation'', now stated in the permutation parts alone, with the phases eliminated entirely.  The one hypothesis a genuinely approximate model does not supply for free is that \(\sigma\) is a homomorphism, and it is worth saying what that costs.  Linearly, with constant one: if the identities the argument uses hold off a set \(B\), then
\[
 \distH\bigl(\widetilde{(d_{[a,b]},\sigma_{[a,b]})},1\bigr)\ \leq\ 2-F_a-F_b+\frac{\abs B}{\abs Y},
\]
and \(B=\emptyset\) is the exact statement.  The two ingredients separate cleanly and each is useful alone: a set of points that are fixed and trivially phased caps the untwisted separation at \(1\) minus its density, and Bonferroni with an exceptional set counts those points.  So the argument does not degrade badly under approximation; the exceptional density enters once, undamped.

The two bounds can now be chained, and the result assumes nothing about untwisting at all.  The commutator argument bounds the trivially phased fixed points of \([a,b]\) from \emph{below}, and the counting bound above---at most half the fixed points of an element are trivially phased---bounds the same set from \emph{above}.  Together,
\[
 2\Bigl(F_a+F_b-1-\tfrac{\abs B}{\abs Y}\Bigr)\ \leq\ F_{[a,b]}+\eps ,
\]
where \(\eps\) bounds the trace at \([a,b]\).  This is a constraint on the permutation parts of \emph{any} monomial model whose trace at \([a,b]\) is small---which is exactly what hyperlinearity supplies---with the phases eliminated and no separation of anything assumed.  Since \(F_{[a,b]}\leq1\) it yields \(F_a+F_b\leq\tfrac32+\tfrac\eps2+\abs B/\abs Y\) outright.

The witnesses do not violate it; they saturate it.  There every permutation part is the identity, so \([a,b]=1\), its trace is \(1\), and the inequality reads \(2(1+1-1)=2=1+1\) on the nose.

It is not obvious that hyperlinearity ever makes \(F_a\) and \(F_b\) large, since separation bounds \(\operatorname{Re}\tau\) and not \(F\).  But the inequality of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK} bounds the trace by the fixed-point fraction, \(\abs{\tau(A_g)}\leq F_g\), and a trace can have large \emph{modulus} while its real part is small---which is exactly the near-scalar case \(\tau\approx-1\) or \(\tau\approx\pm i\) that maximises Hilbert--Schmidt separation.  Chaining,
\[
 \abs{\tau(A_a)}+\abs{\tau(A_b)}\ \leq\ \tfrac32+\tfrac\eps2+\frac{\abs B}{\abs Y},
\]
so two elements cannot both lie within \(3/4\) of the scalars unless their commutator fails to separate.  That is the general form of the two-involution statement of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse}: what obstructs is not the order two but the non-commuting.

The inequality is better read the other way round, as a lower bound on the commutator's trace rather than an upper bound on a sum.  Instantiating the trace bound at its own value and using \(F_{[a,b]}\leq1\),
\[
 \operatorname{Re}\tau\bigl(A_{[a,b]}\bigr)\ \geq\ 2F_a+2F_b-3-\frac{2\abs B}{\abs Y},
\]
so if two elements are both near the scalars---\(\abs{\tau_a},\abs{\tau_b}\approx1\), hence \(F_a,F_b\approx1\)---then the right side is \(\approx1\) and \(A_{[a,b]}\) is as close to the identity as the Hilbert--Schmidt metric can report.  \emph{Near-scalar elements have near-trivial commutators.}

So the near-scalar elements of a monomial model form an approximately abelian set.  That is the structural reason the scalars in a construction like Thom's live in the \emph{centre}: not a design choice but the only place a model can put them, and it says the obstruction of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK} is where it is for a reason internal to the models rather than to the group.

One caution about the chain, since it now carries a compatibility hypothesis, a goodness hypothesis and a trace hypothesis at once.  A theorem whose hypotheses cannot all hold is vacuously true and is caught by nothing: the machine checks guard \emph{definitions} that nothing satisfies, not hypothesis sets that nothing satisfies.  So an instance is exhibited where all three hold simultaneously---two points, trivial action, trivial phases---and there the inequality is an \emph{equality}, both sides being \(4\).  The chain is therefore neither vacuous nor lossy at its extreme.

Finally, the scope of all of this should be stated plainly, because it is easy to lose sight of.  Every result of this remark, and of the four preceding ones on rounding, correction, the scalar class and rational characters, is about \emph{monomial} models.  That is the right class for the sofic side---soficity \emph{is} combinatorial monomial approximability, by the characterization of Remark~\ref{rem:thomK}---but it is not the right class for the hyperlinear side, which supplies arbitrary unitaries.  The monomial matrices are a proper subgroup of \(U(n)\), and not merely proper: the two-point Hadamard matrix is unitary and sits at normalized Hilbert--Schmidt distance at least \(1/4\) from \emph{every} monomial matrix, so it cannot be approximated by monomial data either.

That statement is in a \emph{fixed} basis, and the qualification matters: by the spectral theorem every single unitary is diagonal in some basis, hence monomial there, so the witness does not show that a model resists being conjugated into monomial form.  What it shows is that the monomial matrices are metrically isolated inside \(U(n)\) for a fixed indexing of the model---which is what the phase results assume, and is therefore the right statement for bounding them, but it is not an obstruction to monomialization.  The genuine question is whether a whole \emph{family} \(\set{U_g}\) can be simultaneously monomialized.  The seed of an obstruction is that monomial matrices with trivial permutation part are diagonal and commute, so two non-commuting unitaries are never simultaneously diagonal; permutation parts give the monomial group more room than that, but the real obstruction is available and is basis-free.  The permutation part of a monomial matrix is multiplicative, so a monomial matrix raised to \(\abs{\Sym(Y)}=n!\) has trivial permutation part and is \emph{diagonal}; diagonal matrices commute, whence
\[
 U,V\ \text{monomial in a common basis}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad U^{n!}V^{n!}=V^{n!}U^{n!}.
\]
Both hypothesis and conclusion are conjugation-invariant, so two unitaries whose \(n!\)-th powers fail to commute cannot be simultaneously monomialized in \emph{any} basis.  That is the statement the single-matrix witness could not supply, a lone unitary being diagonal in its own eigenbasis; the exponent \(n!\) is the crude bound \(\abs{\Sym(Y)}\), and the exponent of \(\Sym(Y)\) would serve.

It is worth saying at once what this does \emph{not} give, since the temptation is obvious.  One would like to lift it to groups: if \([g^N,h^N]\neq1\) for every \(N\), must a monomial model fail?  It does not follow, and the reason is the recurring one---\(n!\) is the size of \(\Sym(Y)\), so it grows with the \emph{model}, while a window is fixed before the model is chosen, and reaching \(U_{g^{n!}}\) from \(U_g\) would need \(n!\) multiplications inside the window.  Worse for the hope, the criterion is \emph{vacuous} on the side one would want it to decide: a sofic model is a permutation model, and a permutation matrix raised to \(\abs{\Sym(Y)}\) is the identity, so its \(n!\)-th powers commute for free.  The criterion separates monomial from general unitary matrices, which is what the scope claim needs, and says nothing about whether a group is sofic.

One implication does run in the direction Question~3.4 asks about, and it has been available since the coprime vanishing without being assembled.  Every separation result above is negative for a single reason: the trace bounds \(\abs\tau\leq F\) the wrong way round, so a small trace never forces the fixed-point density down.  When the phase is \emph{trivial on the fixed points}---which \(\gcd(n,m)=1\) forces---no cancellation is available there and the trace is not merely bounded by the fixed-point density but \emph{equal} to it,
\[
 \tau\bigl(A(d,\sigma)\bigr)\ =\ F_\sigma ,
\]
a nonnegative real.  Hyperlinear separation says \(\operatorname{Re}\tau\leq\eps\), and that is now a bound on \(F\) itself; untwisting then returns a model separated to within \(\eps\).  So on a window where every element's order is prime to the modulus, a monomial hyperlinear model \emph{is} a sofic model, with no loss.  The hypothesis is strong---it is precisely the regime in which the phases were shown to be powerless, so the model was never projective in any useful sense---but it is the one place here where hyperlinearity forces soficity rather than merely permitting it.

The trivial-phase hypothesis is more than the argument needs, and weakening it locates the exact boundary.  What was used is only that the phases at fixed points cannot cancel, which is a condition on their real parts: if every such phase has real part at least \(c>0\)---equivalently the phases lie in the open arc of half-angle less than \(\pi/2\) about \(1\)---then \(\operatorname{Re}\tau\geq c\,F_\sigma\), and hyperlinear separation gives
\[
 F_\sigma\ \leq\ \eps/c
\]
outright.  Trivial phases are the case \(c=1\).  Once the phases reach the imaginary axis cancellation begins and the trace stops seeing \(F\) at all: the \(\pm i\) witness sits precisely there, with \(c=0\), trace zero and \(F=1\).  So the positive direction holds exactly as far as the phases stay off the imaginary axis, and fails immediately after.

The two halves were proved far apart and were not aimed at each other, so they are worth recording as one statement.  The trace is linked to the fixed-point density only through \(\abs\tau\leq F\), which runs the wrong way; a trace bound can therefore constrain \(F\) at all only when the phases fail to cancel, and the condition for that is a positive lower bound on their real parts.  Above that threshold hyperlinearity forces soficity, with \(F\leq\eps/c\); at it, it does not, and the failure is realized by a model of trace zero in which \emph{every} point is fixed.  So a group could be hyperlinear through models nowhere near monomial, and none of these results would apply to it.  What justifies the restriction in the one case where it is justified is external---Thom's microstates are monomial because every finite-dimensional unitary representation of \(K\) kills its divisible centre---and that is Remark~\ref{rem:thomK}, not anything proved here.

The extreme case needs no pigeonhole.  On the \emph{same} permutation \(\sigma\), the constant phase \(1\) untwists to a model of separation exactly \(1\), and the constant phase \(0\) untwists to one of separation exactly \(1-F\); and the two monomial matrices differ by a scalar, so they sit at Hilbert--Schmidt distance \(\abs{1-\zeta}^2\), which tends to \(0\) as \(m\) grows.  Two models arbitrarily close in the metric that hyperlinearity uses therefore have untwisted separations \(1\) and \(1-F\) for whatever \(F\) one likes.

All of this traces back to one contrast between the two groups being approximated, worth stating on its own.  \(U(Y)\) has a central element at \emph{maximal} distance from the identity---\(-1\) is scalar, commutes with everything, and \(\norm{-1-1}^2_{\mathrm{HS}}=4\)---while \(\Sym(Y)\) has no nontrivial central element at all once \(\abs Y\geq3\), so every central permutation sits at Hamming distance \(0\).  The unitary group can separate a central element and the symmetric group cannot.  That is why a hyperlinear model may place a central group element at a scalar and be maximally separated there, while a sofic model has nowhere to put it; every phase phenomenon in these remarks is downstream of that asymmetry.

\emph{The metric data does not determine the untwisted separation.}  That is the separation-side counterpart of Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle}, and it says why none of the correction machinery bears on this half: the difficulty of Question~3.4 is not that the phases are hard to make multiplicative, since they are not, but that multiplicativity and separation are controlled by different data and the metric supplies only the first.

For a \emph{torsion} element the scalar freedom disappears, and what is left is stronger.  The phases restrict to a homomorphism on the stabilizer of a point---if \(\sigma_g y=y\) and \(\sigma_h y=y\) then \(d_{gh}(y)=d_g(y)+d_h(y)\), since \(\sigma_h\) does not move \(y\)---so for an involution \(g\) the value \(d_g(y)\) is pinned to the \(2\)-torsion of \(\Z/m\) at every \(y\in\Fix(\sigma_g)\).  It therefore takes at most two values there, and the two contribute opposite signs to the trace.  A trace bound \(E\) then \emph{forces} the two classes to be balanced rather than merely permitting it, so the class of \(0\) holds at least half the fixed points less \(E/2\), and
\[
 \distH\bigl(\widetilde{(d_g,\sigma_g)},1\bigr)\ \leq\ 1-\tfrac12\Bigl(F-\tfrac{E}{\abs Y}\Bigr).
\]
No normalization is chosen anywhere in that statement.  Untwisting an involution inherits its separation and halves it; it cannot create it.  This is the sharp form of the phenomenon, and it is the reason the involutions of Remark~\ref{rem:phasecollapse} keep reappearing as the obstruction.

There is also a bound in the other direction, holding at every order, and it is what makes the involution extremal.  A trivially phased fixed point contributes \(+1\) to the trace, and every other fixed point contributes at least \(-1\), since the phases are unimodular.  A trace bound \(\eps\) is therefore a \emph{counting} bound:
\[
 \abs{\set{y\suchthat\sigma y=y,\ d(y)=1}}\ \leq\ \tfrac12\bigl(\abs{\Fix(\sigma)}+\eps\abs Y\bigr),
\]
with no hypothesis on the torsion whatever.  Untwisting always removes at least half the fixed points; an involution removes exactly half; so the two bounds meet, and the involution is the worst case rather than merely a bad one.

The order-four case shows that this is genuinely about torsion and not about how much the permutation moves.  Take the same two-point model with the identity permutation---the \emph{least} separated permutation part possible---and phases \(\pm i\) rather than \(\pm1\).  The trace is again \(0\), but now the phase never vanishes, so the untwisted permutation is fixed-point free and the untwisted model is maximally separated.  With \(\pm1\) it keeps half its points fixed, attaining the involution bound.  Cancellation buys nothing at order two and everything at order four, and the two witnesses differ in nothing else.

Order three is the remaining case, and it forces retention as well---of a third rather than a half.  The reason is that the cube roots of unity are \emph{positively} dependent: \(1+\omega+\omega^2=0\) is their only relation over \(\R\), so a nonnegative combination \(n_0+n_1\omega+n_2\omega^2\) is small only when all three coefficients are nearly equal.  Writing \(a=n_0-n_2\) and \(b=n_1-n_2\), the relation gives \(n_0+n_1\omega+n_2\omega^2=a+b\omega\), whose squared modulus is the positive definite form \(a^2-ab+b^2\geq\tfrac12(a^2+b^2)\); a trace bound \(E\) therefore forces \(\abs a,\abs b\leq\sqrt2\,E\) and hence
\[
 n_0+n_1+n_2\ \leq\ 3n_0+3\sqrt2\,E .
\]
At order four the single relation splits into two independent ones, \(1+(-1)=0\) and \(i+(-i)=0\), and a nonnegative combination can vanish while missing the class of \(1\) altogether---which is precisely the witness above.  So the trichotomy is complete: orders two and three force retention of \(\tfrac12\) and \(\tfrac13\), and order four and beyond force nothing.

The order is not quite the right invariant, though, and naming the right one tidies the whole classification.  What the phase of an order-\(n\) element can do at a point it fixes is decided by the subgroup of \(\Z/m\) it is pinned to---the \(n\)-torsion---and that subgroup has exactly \(\gcd(m,n)\) elements, by the annihilator count of Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle} read with its two arguments exchanged.  So the trichotomy is really in \(\gcd(n,m)\): value \(1\) forces the phase to vanish, \(2\) retains a half, \(3\) retains a third, and \(4\) or more forces nothing.

This is not a relabelling.  An involution against an \emph{odd} modulus has \(\gcd(2,m)=1\), so its phase is forced to vanish on its fixed points exactly as a coprime element's is, and untwisting gains nothing whatever---although the element has order two and the involution bound would predict a half.  It is the gcd that the phase sees, not the order, and the two part company precisely when the modulus is chosen incompatibly with the torsion.

Finally, the \(\gcd\geq4\) side is an infinite family rather than one accident at \(4\).  At any even modulus, if \(\zeta^{m/2}=-1\) then
\[
 \zeta^{1}+\zeta^{1+m/2}\ =\ \zeta\bigl(1+\zeta^{m/2}\bigr)\ =\ 0,
\]
so two phase classes with equal weights cancel while \emph{neither} is the trivial class; the two-point model with the identity permutation and phases \(\zeta,-\zeta\) then has trace \(0\) and untwists to a fixed-point-free permutation, for every even \(m\geq4\).  The \(\pm i\) witness is the case \(m=4\).

The odd moduli need unequal weights, and that is not an artifact of the construction: for \emph{prime} \(m\) the numbers \(1,\zeta,\dots,\zeta^{m-1}\) satisfy only the rational relation \(\sum_k\zeta^k=0\), which involves the trivial class, so no rational nonnegative combination of the nontrivial classes can vanish.  The irrational weights are easy to write down.  Weight the two classes adjacent to the trivial one by \(1+1/(2\operatorname{Re}\zeta)\) and every other nontrivial class by \(1\); since \(\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}\zeta^k=-1\) and \(\zeta+\zeta^{m-1}=2\operatorname{Re}\zeta\), the total is \(-1+1=0\).  All the weights are positive exactly when \(\operatorname{Re}\zeta>0\), which for \(\zeta=e^{2\pi i/m}\) says \(m\geq5\).  With the even family this settles \(\gcd\geq4\) at every modulus: the trivial class can always be missed.

The involution is the case \(n=2\) of something general, and the general case says when phases can help at all.  Iterating the stabilizer homomorphism gives \(d_{g^k}(y)=k\,d_g(y)\) at a point fixed by \(g\), so if \(g\) has order \(n\) then \(n\,d_g(y)=0\) and \(d_g(y)\) lies in the \(n\)-torsion of \(\Z/m\), a subgroup of \(\gcd(n,m)\) elements.  When \(\gcd(n,m)=1\) that subgroup is trivial, so the phase is \emph{forced to vanish} on the entire fixed point set: cancellation is not merely unhelpful but unavailable.  The trace of the model is then exactly the fixed point density, the untwisted separation is exactly \(1-F\), and a trace bound \emph{is} a separation bound.

So phases can help only when the modulus shares a factor with the torsion.  That is precisely what a construction like Thom's arranges---\(p\)-power phases set against a \(p\)-power centre, as in Remark~\ref{rem:thomK}---and it explains why the character count of Remark~\ref{rem:scalarcocycle} is indexed by the \(p\)-power torsion rather than by anything one is free to choose.  A monomial model whose phase modulus is prime to the orders occurring in the window carries no cancellation at all, and is a sofic model on the nose.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[A lamp chart is a coordinate chart]\label{rem:coordtransfer}%
\leanverified{\leanmod{Sofic/CoordinateTransfer}{OrbitChartData,trivialOrbitChartData,OrbitChartData.comap,lampEmbedding_injective}}%
A referee audit of this development observed that the action-level route to hyperlinearity of a generalized wreath product cannot help a candidate whose coordinate action is not already sofic, because the lamp data \emph{contains} the coordinate data.  The observation is written there against two papers' definitions, but its content depends on neither.  Encode a coordinate \(x\) as the lamp \(\delta_x(k_0)\) for a fixed \(k_0\neq1\).  That encoding is injective, and it is equivariant; consequently charts for the lamp action restrict along it to charts for the coordinate action, with the covariance identity transporting verbatim and nothing used about where the charts take their values.

Both hypotheses are isolated as such here: \(x\mapsto\delta_x(k_0)\) is injective for every nonzero lamp value, and it intertwines the coordinate action with the site action.  The transfer is then a pullback of chart data along an injective equivariant map, valid for charts into any class whatever.  The reading is the audit's: if the coordinate action admits no chart system, neither does the lamp action.  This closes a route to hyperlinearity; it says nothing about whether such a wreath product is hyperlinear by other means, and nothing here says it is.
\end{remark}

\clearpage
\begin{remark}[Two obstructions beyond elementwise approximation]\label{rem:implementer}%
\leanverified{\leanmod{Sofic/ImplementerCocycle}{implementerCocycle,implementerCocycle_twisted,correctedImplementer_defect,hsDistSq_orthogonal_rankOne}}%
Two cautions, both due to an external referee audit of this development and formalized here because they are exactly the kind of statement that should not travel as prose.  Neither bears on Theorem~\ref{thm:A}; both bear on how a unitary model of a wreath-type candidate could be built.

\emph{Implementer coherence is a cocycle problem.}  Suppose an action is implemented elementwise: for each \(g\) a unitary \(v_g\) with \(\operatorname{Ad}(v_g)\) the required automorphism.  Doing this for every \(g\) separately is not the same as having a representation, and the failure is measured by \(c(g,h)=v_gv_hv_{gh}^{-1}\), which satisfies the twisted identity
\[
 c(g,h)\,c(gh,k)=\bigl(v_g\,c(h,k)\,v_g^{-1}\bigr)\,c(g,hk),
\]
while correcting the implementers by \(b_g\) replaces \(c\) by a coboundary: \(g\mapsto b_gv_g\) is multiplicative at \((g,h)\) exactly when \(b_g\,(v_gb_hv_g^{-1})\,c(g,h)\,b_{gh}^{-1}=1\).  So ``each automorphism is approximately inner'' is incomplete data: a construction choosing implementers one element at a time still owes the trivialization of a class.  The calibration is the Pauli pair, where \(\operatorname{Ad}X\) and \(\operatorname{Ad}Z\) commute on \(M_2(\mathbb{C})\) because \(XZ=-ZX\), both are inner, and no commuting implementers exist.  Everything above is a group identity and is stated at that level.

\emph{Normalized rank blindness.}  Two \emph{orthogonal} rank-one projections on a model of size \(n\) sit at normalized squared Hilbert--Schmidt distance \(2/n\), which tends to zero although the projections are as different as two projections can be.  Any argument that tracks a distinguished vector, transports a Kazhdan fixed vector, or preserves an orbit chart must therefore first bound the \emph{relative trace} of the support it tracks; normalized-rank information alone will not do it.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Two compressors, one image]\label{rem:twocompressors}
The pair \(Q=\{u,v\}\) resolves a genuine tension: generation of \(G\) requires elements that move \(\Gamma\), while compression requires elements that map \(\Gamma\) into itself.  The present realization divides the two roles.  The compressor \(u\) seats \(K=u\Gamma u^{-1}\); the involution \(z=vu^{-1}\) generates the missing last row and column of \(\EL_n\) (Proposition~\ref{prop:gen}); and the two compressors share the single image \(K\), so the criterion sees one coherent compression while \(z\) carries the generation.  The criterion itself is indifferent to the size of \(Q\); the algebra here simply supplies two natural compressors rather than one.
\end{remark}
